[![Page semi-protected](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/1b/Semi-protection-shackle.svg/20px-Semi-protection-shackle.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Protection_policy#semi "This article is semi-protected.")
From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia
Attributing events to improbable causes
For other uses, see [Conspiracy theory (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory_\(disambiguation\) "Conspiracy theory \(disambiguation\)").
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Dollarnote_siegel_hq.jpg/330px-Dollarnote_siegel_hq.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dollarnote_siegel_hq.jpg)As seen on the US$1 bill, the [Eye of Providence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eye_of_Providence "Eye of Providence") has been perceived by some to be evidence of a conspiracy linking the [Founding Fathers of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States "Founding Fathers of the United States") to the [New World Order conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World_Order_conspiracy_theory "New World Order conspiracy theory").[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2003-1): 58 [[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-2): 47–49 
A **conspiracy theory** is an explanation for an event or situation that asserts the existence of a [conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy "Conspiracy") (generally by powerful sinister groups, often political in motivation),[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Harambam-Aupers_2021-3)[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-4)[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-5) when other explanations are more probable.[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Harambam-Aupers_2021-3)[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-BrothertonFrench2013-6)[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-probability-7) The term generally has a negative [connotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connotation "Connotation"), as it can often be based in [prejudice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prejudice "Prejudice"), emotional conviction, insufficient evidence or [paranoia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paranoia "Paranoia").[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Byford-8) A conspiracy theory is distinct from a conspiracy; it refers to a hypothesized conspiracy with specific characteristics, including but not limited to opposition to the mainstream consensus among those who are qualified to evaluate its accuracy, such as [scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientist "Scientist") or [historians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historian "Historian").[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9)[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2016-10)[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-q-11) As such, conspiracy theories are identified as [lay theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lay_theory "Lay theory"). 
Conspiracy theories are generally designed to resist [falsification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability "Falsifiability") either by evidence against them or a lack of evidence for them.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-12) They are reinforced by [circular reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circular_reasoning "Circular reasoning"): both evidence against the conspiracy _and_ absence of evidence for it are misinterpreted as evidence of its truth.[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Byford-8)[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Keeley1999-13) Psychologist [Stephan Lewandowsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephan_Lewandowsky "Stephan Lewandowsky") observes "the stronger the evidence against a conspiracy, the more the conspirators must want people to believe their version of events."[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-14) As a consequence, the conspiracy becomes a matter of faith rather than something that can be proven or disproven.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2003-1)[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2011-15) Studies have linked belief in conspiracy theories to [distrust of authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-authority "Anti-authority") and political [cynicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynicism_\(contemporary\) "Cynicism \(contemporary\)").[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-16)[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-17)[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-18) Some researchers suggest that **conspiracist ideation** —belief in conspiracy theories—may be psychologically harmful or pathological.[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Freeman_595%E2%80%93604-19)[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barron_156%E2%80%93159-20) Such belief is correlated with [psychological projection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_projection "Psychological projection"), paranoia, and [Machiavellianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machiavellianism_\(psychology\) "Machiavellianism \(psychology\)").[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-21)[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-22)
Belief in a single conspiracy theory is often associated with belief in other conspiracy theories.[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SuttonDouglas2020-24)[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-annurev-psych-25) Psychologists usually attribute belief in conspiracy theories to a number of psychopathological conditions such as paranoia, [schizotypy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schizotypy "Schizotypy"), [narcissism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcissism "Narcissism"), and [insecure attachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insecure_attachment "Insecure attachment"),[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9) or to a form of [cognitive bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_bias "Cognitive bias") called "[illusory pattern perception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illusory_pattern_perception "Illusory pattern perception")".[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-26)[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-27) It has also been linked with the so-called [Dark triad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_triad "Dark triad") personality types, whose common feature is lack of [empathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empathy "Empathy").[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-28) However, a 2020 review article found that most [cognitive scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_scientist "Cognitive scientist") view conspiracy theorizing as typically nonpathological, given that unfounded belief in conspiracy is common across both historical and contemporary cultures, and may arise from innate human tendencies towards gossip, group cohesion, and religion.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9) One historical review of conspiracy theories concluded that "Evidence suggests that the aversive feelings that people experience when in crisis—fear, uncertainty, and the feeling of being out of control—stimulate a motivation to make sense of the situation, increasing the likelihood of perceiving conspiracies in social situations."[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-29)
Historically, conspiracy theories have been closely linked to prejudice, [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda "Propaganda"), [witch hunts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witch_hunt "Witch hunt"), [wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War "War"), and [genocides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide "Genocide").[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-annurev-psych-25)[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30)[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-HR-32)[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-33)[_[excessive citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources#Bundling_citations "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_] They are often strongly believed by the perpetrators of [terrorist attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism "Terrorism"), and were used as justification by [Timothy McVeigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timothy_McVeigh "Timothy McVeigh") and [Anders Breivik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anders_Breivik "Anders Breivik"), as well as by governments such as [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany"), the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States"), the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union"),[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30) and [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories_in_Turkey "Conspiracy theories in Turkey").[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-34) [AIDS denialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_denialism "AIDS denialism") by the government of [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa "South Africa"), motivated by conspiracy theories, caused an estimated 330,000 deaths from AIDS.[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SimelelaVenter2015-35)[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-BurtonGiddy2015-36) [QAnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QAnon "QAnon") and [denialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_denial_movement_in_the_United_States "Election denial movement in the United States") about the [2020 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election "2020 United States presidential election") results led to the [January 6 United States Capitol attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack "January 6 United States Capitol attack"),[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Nature_2021-37)[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Crossley_2021-38)[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-39) and belief in [conspiracy theories about genetically modified foods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GMO_conspiracy_theories "GMO conspiracy theories") led the government of [Zambia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambia "Zambia") to reject food aid during a [famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine "Famine"),[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31) at a time when three million people in the country were suffering from hunger.[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-BrossardShanahan2007-40) Conspiracy theories are a significant obstacle to improvements in [public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health "Public health"),[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-GlickBooth2014-41) encouraging opposition to public health measures such as [vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccination "Vaccination") and [water fluoridation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_fluoridation "Water fluoridation"). They have been linked to outbreaks of [vaccine-preventable diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine-preventable_disease "Vaccine-preventable disease").[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-GlickBooth2014-41)[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-PrematungeCorace2012-42) Other effects of conspiracy theories include reduced trust in [scientific evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_evidence "Scientific evidence"),[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-annurev-psych-25)[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43) radicalization and ideological reinforcement of [extremist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extremist "Extremist") groups,[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30)[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2015-2-44) and negative consequences for the [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy "Economy").[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30)
Conspiracy theories that were once limited to fringe audiences have become commonplace on the [Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet "Internet") and [social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media "Social media"),[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9)[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-annurev-psych-25) emerging as a [cultural phenomenon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_phenomenon "Cultural phenomenon") of the late 20th and early 21st centuries.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun200358-45)[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Camp_1997-46)[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goldberg_2001-47)[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Fenster_2008-48) They are widespread around the world and are often widely believed, sometimes even by the majority of the population.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49)[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2015-i-51) Interventions to reduce the occurrence of conspiracy beliefs include maintaining an [open society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_society "Open society"), encouraging people to use [analytical thinking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytical_thinking "Analytical thinking"), and reducing feelings of uncertainty, anxiety, or powerlessness.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43)[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49)[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52)
## Origin and usage
The _[Oxford English Dictionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_English_Dictionary "Oxford English Dictionary")_ defines _conspiracy theory_ as "the theory that an event or phenomenon occurs as a result of a conspiracy between interested parties; _spec._ a belief that some covert but influential agency (typically political in motivation and oppressive in intent) is responsible for an unexplained event". The earliest usage example it cites is a 1909 article in _[The American Historical Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_American_Historical_Review "The American Historical Review")_ ,[[53]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-53)[[54]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-54) although the phrase appeared in print several decades before.[[55]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-JoMS-1870-55)
The earliest known usage was by the American author [Charles Astor Bristed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Astor_Bristed "Charles Astor Bristed"), in a letter to the editor published in _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_ on 11 January 1863.[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-nyt-56) He used it to refer to claims that British aristocrats were intentionally [weakening the United States during the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_and_the_American_Civil_War "United Kingdom and the American Civil War") in order to advance their financial interests. 
> England has had quite enough to do in Europe and Asia, without going out of her way to meddle with America. It was a physical and moral impossibility that she could be carrying on a gigantic conspiracy against us. But our masses, having only a rough general knowledge of foreign affairs, and not unnaturally somewhat exaggerating the space which we occupy in the world's eye, do not appreciate the complications which rendered such a conspiracy impossible. They only look at the sudden right-about-face movement of the English Press and public, which is most readily accounted for on the conspiracy theory.[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-nyt-56)
The term is also used as a way to discredit [dissenting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissenting_opinion "Dissenting opinion") analyses.[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-57) Robert Blaskiewicz comments that examples of the term were used as early as the nineteenth century and states that its usage has always been derogatory.[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-CSICOP-58) According to a study by Andrew McKenzie-McHarg, in contrast, in the nineteenth century the term _conspiracy theory_ simply "suggests a plausible postulate of a conspiracy" and "did not, at this stage, carry any connotations, either negative or positive", though sometimes a postulate so-labeled was criticized.[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-59) The author and activist [George Monbiot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Monbiot "George Monbiot") argued that the terms "conspiracy theory" and "conspiracy theorist" are misleading, as conspiracies truly exist and [theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theories "Theories") are "rational explanations subject to disproof". Instead, he proposed the terms "conspiracy fiction" and "conspiracy fantasist".[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-60)
Theories involving multiple conspirators that are proven to be correct such as the [Watergate scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_scandal "Watergate scandal") are usually referred to as [investigative journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investigative_journalism "Investigative journalism") or [historical analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_analysis "Historical analysis") rather than conspiracy theory.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-knight-2003-61) Bjerg (2016) writes: "the way we normally use the term conspiracy theory excludes instances where the theory has been generally accepted as true. The Watergate scandal serves as the standard reference."[[62]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-bjerg1-62) By contrast, the term "Watergate conspiracy theory" is used to refer to a variety of hypotheses in which those convicted in the conspiracy were in fact the victims of a deeper conspiracy.[[63]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-63) There are also attempts to analyze the theory of conspiracy theories (conspiracy theory theory) to ensure that the term "conspiracy theory" is used to refer to narratives that have been debunked by experts, rather than as a generalized dismissal.[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-64)
Conspiracy theorists are different than investigative journalists in that journalists rely on evidence from vetted sources and approach investigations free of bias, rather than believing the theory is a matter of fact. 
### Alleged CIA origins
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/WarrenReport-cover1.jpg/250px-WarrenReport-cover1.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:WarrenReport-cover1.jpg)[The Warren Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Warren_Report "The Warren Report")
The term "conspiracy theory" is itself the subject of a conspiracy theory, which posits that the term was popularized by the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA "CIA") in order to discredit conspiratorial believers, particularly critics of the [Warren Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Commission "Warren Commission"), by making them a target of ridicule.[[65]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2015-4-65) In his 2013 book _Conspiracy Theory in America_ , the political scientist Lance deHaven-Smith wrote that the term entered everyday language in the United States after 1964, the year in which the Warren Commission published its findings on the [assassination of John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_John_F._Kennedy "Assassination of John F. Kennedy"), with _The New York Times_ running five stories that year using the term.[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-66)
Michael Butter, a Professor of American Literary and Cultural History at the [University of Tübingen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_T%C3%BCbingen "University of Tübingen") wrote in 2020 that the CIA document _Concerning Criticism of the Warren Report_ , which proponents of the theory use as evidence of CIA motive and intention, does not contain the phrase "conspiracy theory" in the singular, and only uses the term "conspiracy theories" once, in the sentence: "Conspiracy theories have frequently thrown suspicion on our organisation, for example, by falsely alleging that [Lee Harvey Oswald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Harvey_Oswald "Lee Harvey Oswald") worked for us."[[67]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-67)
Recorded use of the phrase "conspiracy theory" dates back to at least 1863, and it was notably invoked in reports following the 1881 shooting of then-President James A. Garfield, more than 60 years before the CIA was established. An academic review of the digital library JSTOR found the term "conspiracy theorist" had been published at least in the year before Kennedy’s death.[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-68)
## Difference from conspiracy
A conspiracy theory is not simply a [conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy "Conspiracy"), which refers to any covert plan involving two or more people.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2016-10) In contrast, the term "conspiracy theory" refers to _hypothesized_ conspiracies that have specific characteristics. For example, conspiracist beliefs invariably oppose the mainstream consensus among those people who are qualified to evaluate their accuracy, such as [scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientist "Scientist") or [historians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historian "Historian").[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-q-11) Conspiracy theorists see themselves as having privileged access to socially persecuted knowledge or a stigmatized mode of thought that separates them from the masses who believe the official account.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2016-10) [Michael Barkun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Barkun "Michael Barkun") describes a conspiracy theory as a "template imposed upon the world to give the appearance of order to events".[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2016-10)
Real conspiracies, even very simple ones, are difficult to conceal and routinely experience unexpected problems.[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69) In contrast, conspiracy theories suggest that conspiracies are unrealistically successful and that groups of conspirators, such as [bureaucracies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureaucracies "Bureaucracies"), can act with near-perfect competence and secrecy. The causes of events or situations are simplified to exclude complex or interacting factors, as well as the role of chance and unintended consequences. Nearly all observations are explained as having been deliberately planned by the alleged conspirators.[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69)
In conspiracy theories, the conspirators are usually claimed to be acting with extreme malice.[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69) As described by Robert Brotherton: 
> The malevolent intent assumed by most conspiracy theories goes far beyond everyday plots borne out of self-interest, corruption, cruelty, and criminality. The postulated conspirators are not merely people with selfish agendas or differing values. Rather, conspiracy theories postulate a black-and-white world in which good is struggling against evil. The general public is cast as the victim of organised persecution, and the motives of the alleged conspirators often verge on pure maniacal evil. At the very least, the conspirators are said to have an almost inhuman disregard for the basic liberty and well-being of the general population. More grandiose conspiracy theories portray the conspirators as being Evil Incarnate: of having caused all the ills from which we suffer, committing abominable acts of unthinkable cruelty on a routine basis, and striving ultimately to subvert or destroy everything we hold dear.[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69)
## Examples
Further information: [List of conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conspiracy_theories "List of conspiracy theories")
A conspiracy theory may take any matter as its subject, but certain subjects attract greater interest than others. Favored subjects include famous deaths and assassinations, morally dubious government activities, suppressed technologies, and "[false flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag "False flag")" terrorism. Among the best-known conspiracy theories relate to the [assassination of John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_assassination_conspiracy_theories "John F. Kennedy assassination conspiracy theories"), the [1969 Apollo Moon landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing_conspiracy_theories "Moon landing conspiracy theories"), and the [9/11 terrorist attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_conspiracy_theories "9/11 conspiracy theories"), as well as numerous theories pertaining to alleged plots for world domination by various groups, both real and imaginary.[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-70)
## Popularity
Conspiratorial beliefs are widespread around the world.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49) Although scholarly research has focused on conspiracy theories in Western societies, conspiracies have been observed in numerous countries, including in Eastern Europe, Southeast Asia, and the Middle East. In rural Namibia and Tanzania, common targets of conspiracy theorizing include societal elites, enemy tribes, and the Western world, with conspirators often alleged to enact their plans via sorcery or witchcraft; one belief identifies modern technology as itself being a form of sorcery, created with the goal of harming or controlling the people.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49) In China, one widely published conspiracy theory claims that a number of events including the [rise of Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_Hitler "Rise of Hitler"), the [1997 Asian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis "1997 Asian financial crisis"), and [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change "Climate change") were planned by the [Rothschild family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rothschild_family "Rothschild family"), which may have led to effects on discussions about [China's currency policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monetary_policy_of_China "Monetary policy of China").[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Byford2011-71)
Conspiracy theories once limited to fringe audiences have become commonplace in [mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media "Mass media"), contributing to conspiracism emerging as a [cultural phenomenon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_phenomenon "Cultural phenomenon") in the United States of the late 20th and early 21st centuries.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun200358-45)[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Camp_1997-46)[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goldberg_2001-47)[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Fenster_2008-48) The general predisposition to believe conspiracy theories cuts across partisan and ideological lines. Conspiratorial thinking is correlated with antigovernmental orientations and a low sense of political efficacy, with conspiracy believers perceiving a governmental threat to individual rights and displaying a deep skepticism that who one votes for really matters.[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-72)
Conspiracy theories are often commonly believed, some even being held by the majority of the population.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49)[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2015-i-51) A broad cross-section of Americans today gives credence to at least some conspiracy theories.[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-73) For instance, a study conducted in 2016 found that 10% of Americans think the [chemtrail conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemtrail_conspiracy_theory "Chemtrail conspiracy theory") is "completely true" and 20–30% think it is "somewhat true".[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Gizearth-74) This puts "the equivalent of 120 million Americans in the 'chemtrails are real' camp".[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Gizearth-74) Belief in conspiracy theories has therefore become a topic of interest for sociologists, psychologists and experts in [folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore "Folklore"). 
Conspiracy theories are widely present on the [Web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Web "World Wide Web") in the form of [blogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blog "Blog") and [YouTube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouTube "YouTube") videos, as well as on [social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media "Social media"). Whether the Web has increased the prevalence of conspiracy theories or not is an open research question.[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-75) The presence and representation of conspiracy theories in [search engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Search_engine "Search engine") results has been monitored and studied, showing significant variation across different topics, and a general absence of reputable, high-quality links in the results.[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-76)
One conspiracy theory that propagated through former U.S. President Barack Obama's time in office[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-77) claimed that he was [born in Kenya, instead of Hawaii where he was actually born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama_citizenship_conspiracy_theories "Barack Obama citizenship conspiracy theories").[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-78) Former governor of Arkansas and political opponent of Obama [Mike Huckabee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Huckabee "Mike Huckabee") made headlines in 2011[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-79) when he, among other members of [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(United_States\) "Republican Party \(United States\)") leadership, continued to question Obama's citizenship status. 
Another conspiracy theory posits that members of the George W. Bush administration had foreknowledge of the 11 September 2001 terrorist attacks on the World Trade Center and Pentagon. 
**Belief in conspiracy theories in the United States, December 2020 – NPR/Ipsos poll, ±3.3%**[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-80) Conspiracy theory  | Believe  | Not sure   
---|---|---  
"A group of Satan-worshipping elites who run a child sex ring are trying to control our politics and media" ([QAnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QAnon "QAnon"))  |  17% |  37%  
"Several mass shootings in recent years were staged hoaxes" ([crisis actor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crisis_actor "Crisis actor") theory)  |  12% |  27%  
[Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama") was not born in the United States ([birtherism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birtherism "Birtherism"))  |  19% |  22%  
[Moon landing conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing_conspiracy_theories "Moon landing conspiracy theories") |  8% |  20%  
[9/11 conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_conspiracy_theories "9/11 conspiracy theories") |  7% |  20%  
## Types
A conspiracy theory can be local or international, focused on single events or covering multiple incidents and entire countries, regions and periods of history.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2016-10) According to [Russell Muirhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Muirhead "Russell Muirhead") and [Nancy Rosenblum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Rosenblum "Nancy Rosenblum"), historically, traditional conspiracism has entailed a "theory", but over time, "conspiracy" and "theory" have become decoupled, as modern conspiracism is often without any kind of theory behind it.[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-81)[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-82)
[Jesse Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Walker "Jesse Walker") (2013) has identified five kinds of conspiracy theories:[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-83)
  * The "Enemy Outside" refers to theories based on figures alleged to be scheming against a community from without.
  * The "Enemy Within" finds the conspirators lurking inside the nation, indistinguishable from ordinary citizens.
  * The "Enemy Above" involves powerful people manipulating events for their own gain.
  * The "Enemy Below" features the lower classes working to overturn the social order.
  * The "Benevolent Conspiracies" are angelic forces that work behind the scenes to improve the world and help people.


[Michael Barkun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Barkun "Michael Barkun") has identified three classifications of conspiracy theory:[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun20036-84)
  * _Event conspiracy theories_. This refers to limited and well-defined events. Examples may include such conspiracy theories as those concerning the [Kennedy assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_assassination_conspiracies "Kennedy assassination conspiracies"), [9/11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11 "9/11"), and the [spread of AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discredited_AIDS_origins_theories "Discredited AIDS origins theories").
  * _Systemic conspiracy theories_. The conspiracy is believed to have broad goals, usually conceived as securing control of a country, a region, or even the entire world. The goals are sweeping, whilst the conspiratorial machinery is generally simple: a single, evil organization implements a plan to infiltrate and subvert existing institutions. This is a common scenario in conspiracy theories that focus on the alleged machinations of [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Jewish_conspiracy "International Jewish conspiracy"), [Freemasons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masonic_conspiracy_theories "Masonic conspiracy theories"), [Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Scare "Red Scare"), or the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vatican_conspiracy_theories "Vatican conspiracy theories").
  * _Superconspiracy theories_. For Barkun, such theories link multiple alleged conspiracies together hierarchically. At the summit is a distant but all-powerful evil force. His cited examples are the ideas of [David Icke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Icke "David Icke") and [Milton William Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milton_William_Cooper "Milton William Cooper").


### Rothbard: shallow vs. deep
[Murray Rothbard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_Rothbard "Murray Rothbard") argues in favor of a model that contrasts "deep" conspiracy theories to "shallow" ones. According to Rothbard, a "shallow" theorist observes an event and asks _[Cui bono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cui_bono "Cui bono")?_ ("Who benefits?"), jumping to the conclusion that a posited beneficiary is responsible for covertly influencing events. On the other hand, the "deep" conspiracy theorist begins with a hunch and then seeks out evidence. Rothbard describes this latter activity as a matter of confirming with certain facts one's initial paranoia.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-85)
## Lack of evidence
Belief in conspiracy theories is generally based not on evidence but on the faith of the believer.[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun20037-86) As such conspiracy theories are identified as [lay theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lay_theory "Lay theory").[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-87)[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-88) [Noam Chomsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noam_Chomsky "Noam Chomsky") contrasts conspiracy theory to [institutional analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_analysis "Institutional analysis"), which focuses mainly on the public, long-term behavior of publicly known institutions, as recorded in, for example, scholarly documents or [mainstream media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainstream_media "Mainstream media") reports.[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-WintonickQu%C3%A9bec\)1994-89) Conspiracy theory conversely posits the existence of secretive coalitions of individuals and speculates on their alleged activities.[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-90)[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-91) Belief in conspiracy theories is associated with biases in reasoning, such as the [conjunction fallacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjunction_fallacy "Conjunction fallacy").[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-BrothertonFrench2014-92)
Clare Birchall at [King's College London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King%27s_College_London "King's College London") describes conspiracy theory as a "form of popular knowledge or interpretation".[[a]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-94) The use of the word 'knowledge' here suggests ways in which conspiracy theory may be considered in relation to legitimate modes of knowing.[[b]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-95) The relationship between legitimate and illegitimate knowledge, Birchall claims, is closer than common dismissals of conspiracy theory contend.[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-96)
## Rhetoric
Conspiracy theory rhetoric exploits several important [cognitive biases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_bias "Cognitive bias"), including [proportionality bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportionality_bias "Proportionality bias"), [attribution bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attribution_bias "Attribution bias"), and [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias").[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23) Their arguments often take the form of asking reasonable questions, but without providing an answer based on strong evidence.[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-97) Conspiracy theories are most successful when proponents can gather followers from the general public, such as in politics, religion and journalism. These proponents may not necessarily believe the conspiracy theory; instead, they may just use it in an attempt to gain public approval. Conspiratorial claims can act as a successful rhetorical strategy to convince a portion of the public via [appeal to emotion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeal_to_emotion "Appeal to emotion").[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)
Conspiracy theories typically justify themselves by focusing on gaps or ambiguities in knowledge, and then arguing that the true explanation for this [must be a conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argument_from_ignorance "Argument from ignorance").[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69) In contrast, any evidence that directly supports their claims is generally of low quality. For example, conspiracy theories are often dependent on [eyewitness testimony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyewitness_testimony "Eyewitness testimony"), despite its unreliability, while disregarding objective analyses of the evidence.[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69)
Conspiracy theories are not able to be [falsified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsified "Falsified") and are reinforced by [fallacious arguments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallacious_arguments "Fallacious arguments"). In particular, the logical fallacy [circular reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circular_reasoning "Circular reasoning") is used by conspiracy theorists: both evidence against the conspiracy and an absence of evidence for it are re-interpreted as evidence of its truth,[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Byford-8)[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Keeley1999-13) whereby the conspiracy becomes a matter of faith rather than something that can be proved or disproved.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2003-1)[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barkun2011-15) The epistemic strategy of conspiracy theories has been called "cascade logic": each time new evidence becomes available, a conspiracy theory is able to dismiss it by claiming that even more people must be part of the cover-up.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69) Any information that contradicts the conspiracy theory is suggested to be disinformation by the alleged conspiracy.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43) Similarly, the continued lack of evidence directly supporting conspiracist claims is portrayed as confirming the existence of a conspiracy of silence; the fact that other people have not found or exposed any conspiracy is taken as evidence that those people are part of the plot, rather than considering that it may be because no conspiracy exists.[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69) This strategy lets conspiracy theories insulate themselves from neutral analyses of the evidence, and makes them resistant to questioning or correction, which is called "epistemic self-insulation".[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/False_balance_in_climate_science.png/330px-False_balance_in_climate_science.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:False_balance_in_climate_science.png)In 2013, 97% of peer-reviewed climate science papers that took a position on the cause of global warming said that humans are responsible, 3% said they were not. Among [Fox News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News "Fox News") guests the same year, this was presented as a false balance between the two viewpoints, with 31% of invited guests believing it was happening and 69% not.[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-98)
Conspiracy theorists often take advantage of [false balance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_balance "False balance") in the media. They may claim to be presenting a legitimate alternative viewpoint that deserves equal time to argue its case; for example, this strategy has been used by the [Teach the Controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teach_the_Controversy "Teach the Controversy") campaign to promote [intelligent design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligent_design "Intelligent design"), which often claims that there is a conspiracy of scientists suppressing their views. If they successfully find a platform to present their views in a debate format, they focus on using rhetorical _[ad hominems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad_hominem "Ad hominem")_ and attacking perceived flaws in the mainstream account, while avoiding any discussion of the shortcomings in their own position.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)
The typical approach of conspiracy theories is to challenge any action or statement from authorities, using even the most tenuous justifications. Responses are then assessed using a double standard, where failing to provide an immediate response to the satisfaction of the conspiracy theorist will be claimed to prove a conspiracy. Any minor errors in the response are heavily emphasized, while deficiencies in the arguments of other proponents are generally excused.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)
In science, conspiracists may suggest that a [scientific theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_theory "Scientific theory") can be disproven by a single perceived deficiency, even though such events are extremely rare. In addition, both disregarding the claims and attempting to address them will be interpreted as proof of a conspiracy.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31) Other conspiracist arguments may not be scientific; for example, in response to the [IPCC Second Assessment Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPCC_Second_Assessment_Report "IPCC Second Assessment Report") in 1996, much of the opposition centered on promoting a procedural objection to the report's creation. Specifically, it was claimed that part of the procedure reflected a conspiracy to silence dissenters, which served as motivation for opponents of the report and successfully redirected a significant amount of the public discussion away from the science.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)
## Consequences
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Das_j%C3%BCdische_Komplott_Parole_der_Woche_December_1941_crop.jpg/250px-Das_j%C3%BCdische_Komplott_Parole_der_Woche_December_1941_crop.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Das_j%C3%BCdische_Komplott_Parole_der_Woche_December_1941_crop.jpg)Third Reich Nazi antisemitic propaganda poster entitled _Das jüdische Komplott_ ("The Jewish Conspiracy")
Historically, conspiracy theories have been closely linked to [prejudice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prejudice "Prejudice"), [witch hunts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witch_hunt "Witch hunt"), [wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War "War"), and [genocides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide "Genocide").[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30)[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31) They are often strongly believed by the perpetrators of [terrorist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorist "Terrorist") attacks, and were used as justification by [Timothy McVeigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timothy_McVeigh "Timothy McVeigh"), [Anders Breivik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anders_Breivik "Anders Breivik") and [Brenton Tarrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brenton_Tarrant "Brenton Tarrant"), as well as by governments such as [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany") and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union").[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30) [AIDS denialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_denialism "AIDS denialism") by the government of [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa "South Africa"), motivated by conspiracy theories, caused an estimated 330,000 deaths from AIDS,[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SimelelaVenter2015-35)[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-BurtonGiddy2015-36) while belief in [conspiracy theories about genetically modified foods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GMO_conspiracy_theories "GMO conspiracy theories") led the government of [Zambia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambia "Zambia") to reject food aid during a [famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine "Famine"),[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31) at a time when 3 million people in the country were suffering from [hunger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunger "Hunger").[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-BrossardShanahan2007-40)
Conspiracy theories are a significant obstacle to improvements in [public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health "Public health").[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-GlickBooth2014-41) People who believe in health-related conspiracy theories are less likely to follow [medical advice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_advice "Medical advice"), and more likely to use [alternative medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_medicine "Alternative medicine") instead.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30) Conspiratorial [anti-vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-vaccination "Anti-vaccination") beliefs, such as [conspiracy theories about pharmaceutical companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Pharma_conspiracy_theory "Big Pharma conspiracy theory"), can result in reduced vaccination rates and have been linked to outbreaks of [vaccine-preventable diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine-preventable_disease "Vaccine-preventable disease").[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-PrematungeCorace2012-42)[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-GlickBooth2014-41) Health-related conspiracy theories often inspire resistance to [water fluoridation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_fluoridation "Water fluoridation"), and contributed to the impact of the [Lancet MMR autism fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancet_MMR_autism_fraud "Lancet MMR autism fraud").[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-GlickBooth2014-41)
Conspiracy theories are a fundamental component of a wide range of radicalized and extremist groups, where they may play an important role in reinforcing the ideology and psychology of their members as well as further radicalizing their beliefs.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30)[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2015-2-44) These conspiracy theories often share common themes, even among groups that would otherwise be fundamentally opposed, such as the [antisemitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitic "Antisemitic") conspiracy theories found among political extremists on both the [far right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_right "Far right") and [far left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_left "Far left").[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30) More generally, belief in conspiracy theories is associated with holding extreme and uncompromising viewpoints, and may help people in maintaining those viewpoints.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43) While conspiracy theories are not always present in extremist groups, and do not always lead to violence when they are, they can make the group more extreme, provide an enemy to direct hatred towards, and isolate members from the rest of society. Conspiracy theories are most likely to inspire violence when they call for urgent action, appeal to prejudices, or demonize and scapegoat enemies.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2015-2-44)
Conspiracy theorizing in the workplace can also have economic consequences. For example, it leads to lower job satisfaction and lower commitment, resulting in workers being more likely to leave their jobs.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30) Comparisons have also been made with the effects of workplace rumors, which share some characteristics with conspiracy theories and result in both decreased productivity and increased stress. Subsequent effects on managers include reduced profits, reduced trust from employees, and damage to the company's image.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30)[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DiFonzoBordia1994-99)
Conspiracy theories can divert attention from important social, political, and scientific issues.[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Jolley2013-100)[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAm2013-101) In addition, they have been used to discredit scientific evidence to the general public or in a legal context. Conspiratorial strategies also share characteristics with those used by lawyers who are attempting to discredit expert testimony, such as claiming that the experts have ulterior motives in testifying, or attempting to find someone who will provide statements to imply that expert opinion is more divided than it actually is.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel2010-31)
It is possible that conspiracy theories may also produce some compensatory benefits to society in certain situations. For example, they may help people identify governmental deceptions, particularly in repressive societies, and encourage [government transparency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_transparency "Government transparency").[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Jolley2013-100) However, real conspiracies are normally revealed by people working within the system, such as [whistleblowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whistleblower "Whistleblower") and [journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journalist "Journalist"), and most of the effort spent by conspiracy theorists is inherently misdirected.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2015-2-44) The most dangerous conspiracy theories are likely to be those that incite violence, scapegoat disadvantaged groups, or spread [misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misinformation "Misinformation") about important societal issues.[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2015-e-102)
## Interventions
See also: [Misinformation § Countering misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misinformation#Countering_misinformation "Misinformation")
### Target audience
Strategies to address conspiracy theories have been divided into two categories based on whether the target audience is the conspiracy theorists or the general public.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52)[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50) These strategies have been described as reducing [either the supply or the demand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_and_demand "Supply and demand") for conspiracy theories.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50) Both approaches can be used at the same time, although there may be issues of limited resources, or if arguments are used which may appeal to one audience at the expense of the other.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)
Brief scientific literacy interventions, particularly those focusing on critical thinking skills, can effectively undermine conspiracy beliefs and related behaviors. Research led by Penn State scholars, published in the _[Journal of Consumer Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Consumer_Research "Journal of Consumer Research")_ , found that enhancing scientific knowledge and reasoning through short interventions, such as videos explaining concepts like correlation and causation, reduces the endorsement of conspiracy theories. These interventions were most effective against conspiracy theories based on faulty reasoning and were successful even among groups prone to conspiracy beliefs. The studies, involving over 2,700 participants, highlight the importance of educational interventions in mitigating conspiracy beliefs, especially when timed to influence critical decision-making.[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-103)
#### General public
People who feel [empowered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empowered "Empowered") are more resistant to conspiracy theories. Methods to promote empowerment include encouraging people to use [analytical thinking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytical_thinking "Analytical thinking"), [priming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priming_\(psychology\) "Priming \(psychology\)") people to think of situations where they are in control, and ensuring that decisions by society and government are seen to follow procedural fairness (the use of fair decision-making procedures).[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52)
Methods of refutation which have shown effectiveness in various circumstances include: providing facts that demonstrate the conspiracy theory is false, attempting to discredit the source, explaining how the logic is invalid or misleading, and providing links to fact-checking websites.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52) It can also be effective to use these strategies in advance, informing people that they could encounter misleading information in the future, and why the information should be rejected (also called inoculation or prebunking).[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52)[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-ConnollyUscinski2019-104)[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LazicZezelj2021-105) While it has been suggested that discussing conspiracy theories can raise their profile and make them seem more legitimate to the public, the discussion can put people on guard instead as long as it is sufficiently persuasive.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9)
Other approaches to reduce the appeal of conspiracy theories in general among the public may be based in the emotional and social nature of conspiratorial beliefs. For example, interventions that promote analytical thinking in the general public are likely to be effective. Another approach is to intervene in ways that decrease [negative emotions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negative_emotion "Negative emotion"), and specifically to improve feelings of personal hope and empowerment.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49)
#### Conspiracy theorists
It is much more difficult to convince people who already believe in conspiracy theories.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52) Conspiracist belief systems are not based on external evidence, but instead use [circular logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circular_logic "Circular logic") where every belief is supported by other conspiracist beliefs.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52) In addition, conspiracy theories have a "self-sealing" nature, in which the types of arguments used to support them make them resistant to questioning from others.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)
Characteristics of successful strategies for reaching conspiracy theorists have been divided into several broad categories: 1) Arguments can be presented by "trusted messengers", such as people who were formerly members of an extremist group. 2) Since conspiracy theorists think of themselves as people who value critical thinking, this can be affirmed and then redirected to encourage being more critical when analyzing the conspiracy theory. 3) Approaches demonstrate empathy, and are based on building understanding together, which is supported by modeling open-mindedness in order to encourage the conspiracy theorists to do likewise. 4) The conspiracy theories are not attacked with ridicule or aggressive deconstruction, and interactions are not treated like an argument to be won; this approach can work with the general public, but among conspiracy theorists it may simply be rejected.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52)
Interventions that reduce feelings of uncertainty, anxiety, or powerlessness result in a reduction in conspiracy beliefs.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43) Other possible strategies to mitigate the effect of conspiracy theories include education, media literacy, and increasing governmental openness and transparency.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-ConnollyUscinski2019-104) Due to the relationship between conspiracy theories and political extremism, the academic literature on [deradicalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deradicalization "Deradicalization") is also important.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52)
One approach describes conspiracy theories as resulting from a "crippled epistemology", in which a person encounters or accepts very few relevant sources of information.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Hardin2002-106) A conspiracy theory is more likely to appear justified to people with a limited "informational environment" who only encounter misleading information. These people may be "[epistemologically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemologically "Epistemologically") isolated" in [self-enclosed networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filter_bubble "Filter bubble"). From the perspective of people within these networks, disconnected from the information available to the rest of society, believing in conspiracy theories may appear to be justified.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Hardin2002-106) In these cases, the solution would be to break the group's informational isolation.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)
### Reducing transmission
Public exposure to conspiracy theories can be reduced by interventions that reduce their ability to spread, such as by encouraging people to reflect before sharing a news story.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LewandowskyCook2020-52) Researchers Carlos Diaz Ruiz and Tomas Nilsson have proposed technical and rhetorical interventions to counter the spread of conspiracy theories on social media.[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-EC-107)
Interventions to counter the spread of conspiracy theories on social media[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-EC-107) Type of intervention  | Intervention   
---|---  
Technical  | Expose sources that insert and circulate conspiracy theories on social media (flagging).   
Diminish the source's capacity to monetize conspiracies (demonetization).   
Slow down the circulation of conspiracy theories (algorithm)   
Rhetorical  | Issue authoritative corrections (fact-checking).   
Authority-based corrections and fact-checking may backfire because personal worldviews cannot be proved wrong.   
Enlist spokespeople that can be perceived as allies and insiders.   
Rebuttals must spring from an epistemology that participants are already familiar with.   
Give believers of conspiracies an "exit ramp" to dis-invest themselves without facing ridicule.   
### Government policies
The primary defense against conspiracy theories is to maintain an [open society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_society "Open society"), in which many sources of reliable information are available, and government sources are known to be credible rather than propaganda. Additionally, independent nongovernmental organizations are able to correct misinformation without requiring people to trust the government.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50) The absence of [civil rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights "Civil rights") and [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties "Civil liberties") reduces the number of information sources available to the population, which may lead people to support conspiracy theories.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50) Since the credibility of conspiracy theories can be increased if governments act dishonestly or otherwise engage in objectionable actions, avoiding such actions is also a relevant strategy.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-ConnollyUscinski2019-104)
Joseph Pierre has said that mistrust in authoritative institutions is the core component underlying many conspiracy theories and that this mistrust creates an epistemic vacuum and makes individuals searching for answers vulnerable to misinformation. Therefore, one possible solution is offering consumers a seat at the table to mend their mistrust in institutions.[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-108) Regarding the challenges of this approach, Pierre has said, "The challenge with acknowledging areas of uncertainty within a public sphere is that doing so can be weaponized to reinforce a post-truth view of the world in which everything is debatable, and any counter-position is just as valid. Although I like to think of myself as a middle of the road kind of individual, it is important to keep in mind that the truth does not always lie in the middle of a debate, whether we are talking about climate change, vaccines, or antipsychotic medications."[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-109)
Researchers have recommended that public policies should take into account the possibility of conspiracy theories relating to any policy or policy area, and prepare to combat them in advance.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-ConnollyUscinski2019-104)[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9) Conspiracy theories have suddenly arisen in the context of policy issues as disparate as land-use laws and bicycle-sharing programs.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-ConnollyUscinski2019-104) In the case of public communications by government officials, factors that improve the effectiveness of communication include using clear and simple messages, and using messengers which are trusted by the target population. Government information about conspiracy theories is more likely to be believed if the messenger is perceived as being part of someone's [in-group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In-group "In-group"). Official representatives may be more effective if they share characteristics with the target groups, such as ethnicity.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-ConnollyUscinski2019-104)
In addition, when the government communicates with citizens to combat conspiracy theories, online methods are more efficient compared to other methods such as print publications. This also promotes transparency, can improve a message's perceived trustworthiness, and is more effective at reaching underrepresented demographics. However, as of 2019[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Conspiracy_theory&action=edit), many governmental websites do not take full advantage of the available information-sharing opportunities. Similarly, social media accounts need to be used effectively in order to achieve meaningful communication with the public, such as by responding to requests that citizens send to those accounts. Other steps include adapting messages to the communication styles used on the social media platform in question, and promoting a culture of openness. Since mixed messaging can support conspiracy theories, it is also important to avoid conflicting accounts, such as by ensuring the accuracy of messages on the social media accounts of individual members of the organization.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-ConnollyUscinski2019-104)
### Public health campaigns
Successful methods for dispelling conspiracy theories have been studied in the context of [public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health "Public health") campaigns. A key characteristic of communication strategies to address medical conspiracy theories is the use of techniques that rely less on emotional appeals. It is more effective to use methods that encourage people to process information rationally. The use of visual aids is also an essential part of these strategies. Since conspiracy theories are based on intuitive thinking, and visual information processing relies on intuition, visual aids are able to compete directly for the public's attention.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9)
In public health campaigns, information retention by the public is highest for loss-framed messages that include more extreme outcomes. However, excessively appealing to catastrophic scenarios (e.g. low vaccination rates causing an epidemic) may provoke anxiety, which is associated with conspiracism and could increase belief in conspiracy theories instead. [Scare tactics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scare_tactics "Scare tactics") have sometimes had mixed results, but are generally considered ineffective. An example of this is the use of images that showcase disturbing health outcomes, such as the impact of smoking on dental health. One possible explanation is that information processed via the fear response is typically not evaluated rationally, which may prevent the message from being linked to the desired behaviors.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9)
A particularly important technique is the use of [focus groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Focus_group "Focus group") to understand exactly what people believe, and the reasons they give for those beliefs. This allows messaging to focus on the specific concerns that people identify, and on topics that are easily misinterpreted by the public, since these are factors which conspiracy theories can take advantage of. In addition, discussions with focus groups and observations of the group dynamics can indicate which anti-conspiracist ideas are most likely to spread.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9)
Interventions that address medical conspiracy theories by reducing powerlessness include emphasizing the principle of [informed consent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informed_consent "Informed consent"), giving patients all the relevant information without imposing decisions on them, to ensure that they have a sense of control. Improving access to healthcare also reduces medical conspiracism. However, doing so by political efforts can also fuel additional conspiracy theories, which occurred with the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act "Affordable Care Act") (Obamacare) in the United States. Another successful strategy is to require people to watch a short video when they fulfil requirements such as registration for school or a drivers' license, which has been demonstrated to improve vaccination rates and signups for organ donation.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9)
Another approach is based on viewing conspiracy theories as narratives which express personal and cultural values, making them less susceptible to straightforward factual corrections, and more effectively addressed by counter-narratives.[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LazicZezelj2021-105)[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Adornetti2023-110) Counter-narratives can be more engaging and memorable than simple corrections, and can be adapted to the specific values held by individuals and cultures. These narratives may depict personal experiences, or alternatively they can be cultural narratives. In the context of vaccination, examples of cultural narratives include stories about scientific breakthroughs, about the world before vaccinations, or about heroic and altruistic researchers. The themes to be addressed would be those that could be exploited by conspiracy theories to increase [vaccine hesitancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_hesitancy "Vaccine hesitancy"), such as perceptions of vaccine risk, lack of patient empowerment, and lack of trust in medical authorities.[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LazicZezelj2021-105)
### Backfire effects
It has been suggested that directly countering [misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misinformation "Misinformation") can be counterproductive. For example, since conspiracy theories can reinterpret disconfirming information as part of their narrative, refuting a claim can result in accidentally reinforcing it,[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Brotherton2013-69)[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAm2019-111) which is referred to as a "backfire effect".[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Swire-Thompson2020-112) In addition, publishing criticism of conspiracy theories can result in legitimizing them.[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Jolley2013-100) In this context, possible interventions include carefully selecting which conspiracy theories to refute, requesting additional analyses from independent observers, and introducing cognitive diversity into conspiratorial communities by undermining their poor epistemology.[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Jolley2013-100) Any legitimization effect might also be reduced by responding to more conspiracy theories rather than fewer.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SunsteinVermeule2009-50)
There are psychological mechanisms by which backfire effects could potentially occur, but the evidence on this topic is mixed, and backfire effects are very rare in practice.[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-LazicZezelj2021-105)[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Swire-Thompson2020-112)[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Nyhan2021-113) A 2020 review of the scientific literature on backfire effects found that there have been widespread [failures to replicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replication_crisis "Replication crisis") their existence, even under conditions that would be theoretically favorable to observing them.[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Swire-Thompson2020-112) Due to the lack of [reproducibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproducibility "Reproducibility"), as of 2020[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Conspiracy_theory&action=edit) most researchers believe that backfire effects are either unlikely to occur on the broader population level, or they only occur in very specific circumstances, or they do not exist.[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Swire-Thompson2020-112) Brendan Nyhan, one of the researchers who initially proposed the occurrence of backfire effects, wrote in 2021 that the persistence of misinformation is most likely due to other factors.[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Nyhan2021-113)
