In general, people do reject conspiracy theories when they learn about their contradictions and lack of evidence.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Andrade2020-9) For most people, corrections and fact-checking are very unlikely to have a negative impact, and there is no specific group of people in which backfire effects have been consistently observed.[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Swire-Thompson2020-112) Presenting people with factual corrections, or highlighting the logical contradictions in conspiracy theories, has been demonstrated to have a positive effect in many circumstances.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49)[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAm2019-111) For example, this has been studied in the case of informing believers in [9/11 conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_conspiracy_theories "9/11 conspiracy theories") about statements by actual experts and witnesses.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49) One possibility is that criticism is most likely to backfire if it challenges someone's worldview or identity. This suggests that an effective approach may be to provide criticism while avoiding such challenges.[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAm2019-111)
## Psychology
The widespread belief in conspiracy theories has become a topic of interest for sociologists, psychologists, and experts in folklore since at least the 1960s, when [a number of conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_assassination_conspiracy_theories "John F. Kennedy assassination conspiracy theories") arose regarding [the assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_assassination "John F. Kennedy assassination") of U.S. President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy "John F. Kennedy"). [Sociologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociologist "Sociologist") Türkay Salim Nefes underlines the political nature of conspiracy theories. He suggests that one of the most important characteristics of these accounts is their attempt to unveil the "real but hidden" power relations in social groups.[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Turkay_Nefes-114)[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Nefes-115) The term "conspiracism" was popularized by academic Frank P. Mintz in the 1980s. According to Mintz, conspiracism denotes "belief in the primacy of conspiracies in the unfolding of history":[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Mintz1985-116): 4 
> Conspiracism serves the needs of diverse political and social groups in America and elsewhere. It identifies elites, blames them for economic and social catastrophes, and assumes that things will be better once popular action can remove them from positions of power. As such, conspiracy theories do not typify a particular epoch or ideology.[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Mintz1985-116): 199 
Research suggests, on a psychological level, **conspiracist ideation** —belief in conspiracy theories—can be harmful or pathological,[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Freeman_595%E2%80%93604-19)[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Barron_156%E2%80%93159-20) and is highly correlated with [psychological projection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_projection "Psychological projection"), as well as with [paranoia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paranoia "Paranoia"), which is predicted by the degree of a person's [Machiavellianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machiavellianism_\(psychology\) "Machiavellianism \(psychology\)").[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-117) The propensity to believe in conspiracy theories is strongly associated with the mental health disorder of [schizotypy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schizotypy "Schizotypy").[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-118)[[117]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-119)[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-120)[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-121)[[120]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-122) Conspiracy theories once limited to fringe audiences have become commonplace in [mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media "Mass media"), emerging as a [cultural phenomenon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_phenomenon "Cultural phenomenon") of the late 20th and early 21st centuries.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun200358-45)[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Camp_1997-46)[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goldberg_2001-47)[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Fenster_2008-48) Exposure to conspiracy theories in news media and popular entertainment increases receptiveness to conspiratorial ideas, and has also increased the social acceptability of fringe beliefs.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-DouglasUscinski2019-30)[[121]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-StojanovHalberstadt2020-123)
Conspiracy theories often use complicated and detailed arguments, including ones that appear analytical or scientific. However, belief in conspiracy theories is primarily driven by emotion.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-van_ProoijenDouglas2018-49) One of the most widely confirmed facts about conspiracy theories is that belief in a single conspiracy theory is often associated with belief in other conspiracy theories.[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SuttonDouglas2020-24) This even applies when the conspiracy theories directly contradict each other—e.g., believing that [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden "Osama bin Laden") was already dead before his compound in Pakistan was attacked makes the same person more likely to believe that he is still alive. One conclusion from this finding is that the content of a conspiracist belief is less important than the idea of a coverup by the authorities.[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Thresher-Andrews2013-23)[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAm2013-101)[[122]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-BilewiczCichocka2015-124) [Analytical thinking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytical_thinking "Analytical thinking") helps reduce believing in conspiracy theories, in part because it emphasizes rational and critical cognition.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43)
Some psychologists assert that explanations related to conspiracy theories can be, and often are, "internally consistent" with strong beliefs previously held prior to the event that sparked the belief in a conspiracy.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43) People who believe in conspiracy theories tend to believe in other unsubstantiated claims, including [pseudoscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscience "Pseudoscience") and [paranormal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paranormal "Paranormal") phenomena.[[123]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-125)
### Attractions
Psychological motives for believing in conspiracy theories can be categorized as epistemic, existential, or social. These motives are particularly acute in vulnerable and disadvantaged populations. However, it does not appear that the beliefs help to address these motives; in fact, they may be self-defeating, acting to make the situation worse instead.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43)[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAm2019-111) For example, while conspiratorial beliefs can result from a perceived sense of [powerlessness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empowerment "Empowerment"), exposure to conspiracy theories immediately suppresses personal feelings of autonomy and control. Furthermore, they also make people less likely to take actions that could improve their circumstances.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43)[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAm2019-111)
This is additionally supported by the fact that conspiracy theories have a number of disadvantageous attributes.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43) For example, they promote a hostile and distrustful view of other people and groups allegedly acting based on antisocial and cynical motivations. This is expected to lead to increased [social alienation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_alienation "Social alienation") and [anomie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anomie "Anomie") and reduced [social capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_capital "Social capital"). Similarly, they depict the public as ignorant and powerless against the alleged conspirators, with important aspects of society determined by malevolent forces, a viewpoint that is likely to be disempowering.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_538%E2%80%93542-43)
Each person may endorse conspiracy theories for one of many different reasons.[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-GoreisVoracek2019-126) The most consistently demonstrated characteristics of people who find conspiracy theories appealing are a feeling of [alienation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_alienation "Social alienation"), unhappiness or dissatisfaction with their situation, an unconventional worldview, and a sense of [disempowerment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disempowerment "Disempowerment").[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-GoreisVoracek2019-126) While various aspects of personality affect susceptibility to conspiracy theories, none of the [Big Five personality traits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Five_personality_traits "Big Five personality traits") are associated with conspiracy beliefs.[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-GoreisVoracek2019-126)
The political scientist [Michael Barkun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Barkun "Michael Barkun"), discussing the usage of "conspiracy theory" in contemporary American culture, holds that this term is used for a belief that explains an event as the result of a secret plot by exceptionally powerful and cunning conspirators to achieve a malevolent end.[[125]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun20033-127)[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-New_Internationalist_1_2004-128) According to Barkun, the appeal of conspiracism is threefold: 
>   * First, conspiracy theories claim to explain what [institutional analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_analysis "Institutional analysis") cannot. They appear to make sense out of a world that is otherwise confusing.
>   * Second, they do so in an appealingly simple way, by dividing the world sharply between [the forces of light, and the forces of darkness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manichaeism "Manichaeism"). They trace all evil back to a single source, the conspirators and their agents.
>   * Third, conspiracy theories are often presented as special, [secret knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_esotericism "Western esotericism") unknown or unappreciated by others. For conspiracy theorists, the masses are a [brainwashed herd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheeple "Sheeple"), while the conspiracy theorists in the know can congratulate themselves on penetrating the plotters' deceptions.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-New_Internationalist_1_2004-128)
> 

This third point is supported by the research of Roland Imhoff, professor of [social psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_psychology "Social psychology") at the [Johannes Gutenberg University Mainz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Gutenberg_University_Mainz "Johannes Gutenberg University Mainz"). His research suggests that the smaller the minority believing in a specific theory, the more attractive it is to conspiracy theorists.[[127]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-129) [Humanistic psychologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Humanistic_psychologists&action=edit&redlink=1 "Humanistic psychologists \(page does not exist\)") argue that even if a posited cabal behind an alleged conspiracy is almost always perceived as hostile, there often remains an element of reassurance for theorists. This is because it is a consolation to imagine that humans create difficulties in human affairs and remain within human control. If a cabal can be implicated, there may be a hope of breaking its power or of joining it. Belief in the power of a cabal is an implicit assertion of human dignity—an unconscious affirmation that man is responsible for his own destiny.[[128]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Baigent,_Leigh_&_Lincoln_1987-130)
People formulate conspiracy theories to explain, for example, power relations in social groups and the perceived existence of evil forces.[[c]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-131)[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-New_Internationalist_1_2004-128)[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Turkay_Nefes-114)[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Nefes-115) Proposed psychological origins of conspiracy theorizing include projection; the personal need to explain "a significant event [with] a significant cause;" and the product of various kinds and stages of thought disorder, such as paranoid disposition, ranging in severity to diagnosable mental illnesses. Some people prefer socio-political explanations over the insecurity of encountering [random](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Random "Random"), unpredictable, or otherwise inexplicable events.[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-business.time.com-132)[[130]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Goertzel_1994_733%E2%80%93744-133)[[131]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Douglas_2008_210%E2%80%93222-134)[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-harpers=1964-135)[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Hodapp_2008-136)[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Cohen-137) According to Berlet and Lyons, "Conspiracism is a particular narrative form of scapegoating that frames demonized enemies as part of a vast insidious plot against the common good, while it valorizes the scapegoater as a hero for sounding the alarm".[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-138)
### Causes
Some psychologists believe that a search for meaning is common in conspiracism. Once cognized, [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias") and avoidance of [cognitive dissonance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_dissonance "Cognitive dissonance") may reinforce the belief. When a conspiracy theory has become embedded within a social group, [communal reinforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communal_reinforcement "Communal reinforcement") may also play a part.[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-139)
Inquiry into possible motives behind the accepting of irrational conspiracy theories has linked[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-140) these beliefs to distress resulting from an event that occurred, such as the [events of 9/11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_11_attacks "September 11 attacks"). Additional research suggests that "delusional ideation" is the trait most likely to indicate a stronger belief in conspiracy theories.[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-141) Research also shows an increased attachment to these irrational beliefs leads to a decreased desire for civic engagement.[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-BrothertonFrench2014-92) Belief in conspiracy theories is correlated with low intelligence, lower analytical thinking, [anxiety disorders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anxiety_disorder "Anxiety disorder"), [paranoia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paranoia "Paranoia"), and [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian "Authoritarian") beliefs.[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-142)[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-143)[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-144)
Professor [Quassim Cassam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quassim_Cassam "Quassim Cassam") argues that conspiracy theorists hold their beliefs due to flaws in their thinking and, more precisely, their intellectual character. He cites philosopher [Linda Trinkaus Zagzebski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Trinkaus_Zagzebski "Linda Trinkaus Zagzebski") and her book _Virtues of the Mind_ in outlining intellectual virtues (such as humility, caution, and carefulness) and intellectual vices (such as gullibility, carelessness, and closed-mindedness). Whereas intellectual virtues help reach sound examination, intellectual vices "impede effective and responsible inquiry", meaning that those prone to believing in conspiracy theories possess certain vices while lacking necessary virtues.[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-145)
Some researchers have suggested that conspiracy theories could be partially caused by the human brain's mechanisms for detecting dangerous coalitions. Such a mechanism could have been helpful in the small-scale environment humanity evolved in but is mismatched in a modern, complex society and thus "misfire", perceiving conspiracies where none exist.[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-146)
#### Projection
Some historians have argued that [psychological projection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_projection "Psychological projection") is prevalent amongst conspiracy theorists. According to the argument, this projection is manifested in the form of attributing undesirable characteristics of the self to the conspirators. Historian Richard Hofstadter stated that: 
> This enemy seems on many counts a projection of the self; both the ideal and the unacceptable aspects of the self are attributed to him. A fundamental paradox of the paranoid style is the imitation of the enemy. The enemy, for example, may be the cosmopolitan intellectual, but the paranoid will outdo him in the apparatus of scholarship, even of pedantry. ... The Ku Klux Klan imitated Catholicism to the point of donning priestly vestments, developing an elaborate ritual and an equally elaborate hierarchy. The [John Birch Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Birch_Society "John Birch Society") emulates Communist cells and quasi-secret operation through "front" groups, and preaches a ruthless prosecution of the ideological war along lines very similar to those it finds in the Communist enemy. Spokesmen of the various fundamentalist anti-Communist "crusades" openly express their admiration for the dedication, discipline, and strategic ingenuity the Communist cause calls forth.[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-harpers=1964-135)
Hofstadter also noted that "sexual freedom" is a vice frequently attributed to the conspiracist's target group, noting that "very often the fantasies of true believers reveal strong sadomasochistic outlets, vividly expressed, for example, in the delight of anti-Masons with the cruelty of Masonic punishments".[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-harpers=1964-135)
#### Physiology
[Marcel Danesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Danesi "Marcel Danesi") suggests that people who believe conspiracy theories have difficulty rethinking situations. Exposure to those theories has caused neural pathways to be more rigid and less subject to change. Initial susceptibility to believing these theories' lies, dehumanizing language, and metaphors leads to the acceptance of larger and more extensive theories because the hardened neural pathways are already present. Repetition of the "facts" of conspiracy theories and their connected lies simply reinforces the rigidity of those pathways. Thus, conspiracy theories and dehumanizing lies are not mere hyperbole; they can actually change the way people think: 
> Unfortunately, research into this brain wiring also shows that once people begin to believe lies, they are unlikely to change their minds even when confronted with evidence that contradicts their beliefs. It is a form of brainwashing. Once the brain has carved out a well-worn path of believing deceit, it is even harder to step out of that path – which is how fanatics are born. Instead, these people will seek out information that confirms their beliefs, avoid anything that is in conflict with them, or even turn the contrasting information on its head, so as to make it fit their beliefs.
> People with strong convictions will have a hard time changing their minds, given how embedded a lie becomes in the mind. In fact, there are scientists and scholars still studying the best tools and tricks to combat lies with some combination of brain training and linguistic awareness.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-147)
## Sociology
In addition to psychological factors such as conspiracist ideation, sociological factors also help account for who believes in which conspiracy theories. Such theories tend to get more traction among election losers in society, for example, and the emphasis on conspiracy theories by elites and leaders tends to increase belief among followers with higher levels of conspiracy thinking.[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-148) [Christopher Hitchens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Hitchens "Christopher Hitchens") described conspiracy theories as the "exhaust fumes of democracy":[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Hodapp_2008-136) the unavoidable result of a large amount of information circulating among a large number of people. 
Conspiracy theories may be emotionally satisfying, as they assign blame to a group to which the theorist does not belong and, thus, absolve the theorist of moral or political responsibility in society.[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-149) Likewise, [Roger Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Cohen "Roger Cohen") writing for _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_ has said that, "captive minds; ... resort to conspiracy theory because it is the ultimate refuge of the powerless. If you cannot change your own life, it must be that some greater force controls the world."[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Cohen-137)
Sociological historian Holger Herwig found in studying German explanations for the origins of [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I "World War I"), "Those events that are most important are hardest to understand because they attract the greatest attention from myth makers and charlatans."[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-150) [Justin Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Fox "Justin Fox") of _[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time \(magazine\)")_ magazine argues that Wall Street traders are among the most conspiracy-minded group of people, and ascribes this to the reality of some financial market conspiracies, and to the ability of conspiracy theories to provide necessary orientation in the market's day-to-day movements.[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-business.time.com-132)
### Influence of critical theory
[Bruno Latour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Latour "Bruno Latour") notes that the language and intellectual tactics of [critical theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_theory "Critical theory") have been appropriated by those he describes as conspiracy theorists, including [climate-change denialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate-change_denial "Climate-change denial") and the [9/11 truth movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_truth_movement "9/11 truth movement"): "Maybe I am taking conspiracy theories too seriously, but I am worried to detect, in those mad mixtures of knee-jerk disbelief, punctilious demands for proofs, and free use of powerful explanation from the social neverland, many of the weapons of social critique."[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-critiquesteam-151)
### Fusion paranoia
[Michael Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Kelly_\(editor\) "Michael Kelly \(editor\)"), a _[Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Post "Washington Post")_ journalist and critic of [anti-war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-war "Anti-war") movements on both the left and right, coined the term "fusion paranoia" to refer to a political convergence of left-wing and right-wing activists around anti-war issues and [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties "Civil liberties"), which he said were motivated by a shared belief in conspiracism or shared [anti-government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-statism "Anti-statism") views.[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-152)
Barkun has adopted this term to refer to how the synthesis of paranoid conspiracy theories, which were once limited to American fringe audiences, has given them mass appeal and enabled them to become commonplace in [mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media "Mass media"),[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun2003230-153) thereby inaugurating an unrivaled period of people actively preparing for [apocalyptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocalypticism "Apocalypticism") or [millenarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millenarian "Millenarian") scenarios in the United States of the late 20th and early 21st centuries.[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun2003207,_210,_211-154) Barkun notes the occurrence of lone-wolf conflicts with law enforcement acting as a proxy for threatening the established political powers.[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkun2003193,_197-155)
## Viability
As evidence that undermines an alleged conspiracy grows, the number of alleged conspirators also grows in the minds of conspiracy theorists. This is because of an assumption that the alleged conspirators often have competing interests. For example, if Republican President [George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush "George W. Bush") is allegedly responsible for the 9/11 terrorist attacks, and the Democratic party did not pursue exposing this alleged plot, that must mean that both the Democratic and Republican parties are conspirators in the alleged plot. It also assumes that the alleged conspirators are so competent that they can fool the entire world, but so incompetent that even the unskilled conspiracy theorists can find mistakes they make that prove the fraud. At some point, the number of alleged conspirators, combined with the contradictions within the alleged conspirators' interests and competence, becomes so great that maintaining the theory becomes an obvious exercise in absurdity.[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-156)
The physicist [David Robert Grimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Robert_Grimes "David Robert Grimes") estimated the time it would take for a conspiracy to be exposed based on the number of people involved.[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-pbs_plos_one-157)[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Grimes_PLOS_ONE-158) His calculations used data from the [PRISM surveillance program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PRISM_surveillance_program "PRISM surveillance program"), the [Tuskegee syphilis experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_syphilis_experiment "Tuskegee syphilis experiment"), and the [FBI forensic scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI_Laboratory#Controversy "FBI Laboratory"). Grimes estimated that: 
  * A [Moon landing hoax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing_hoax "Moon landing hoax") would require the involvement of 411,000 people and would be exposed within 3.68 years;
  * [Climate-change fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming_conspiracy_theory "Global warming conspiracy theory") would require a minimum of 29,083 people (published climate scientists only) and would be exposed within 26.77 years, or up to 405,000 people, in which case it would be exposed within 3.70 years;
  * A vaccination conspiracy would require a minimum of 22,000 people (without drug companies) and would be exposed within at least 3.15 years and at most 34.78 years depending on the number involved;
  * A conspiracy to [suppress a cure for cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Pharma_conspiracy_theory "Big Pharma conspiracy theory") would require 714,000 people and would be exposed within 3.17 years.


Grimes's study did not consider exposure by sources outside of the alleged conspiracy. It only considered exposure from within the alleged conspiracy through whistleblowers or through incompetence.[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-159) Subsequent comments on the [PubPeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PubPeer "PubPeer") website[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-160) point out that these calculations must exclude successful conspiracies since, by definition, we don't know about them, and are wrong by an order of magnitude about [Bletchley Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bletchley_Park "Bletchley Park"), which remained a secret far longer than Grimes' calculations predicted. 
## Terminology
The term "truth seeker" is adopted by some conspiracy theorists when describing themselves on social media.[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-161) Conspiracy theorists are often referred to derogatorily as "[cookers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooker_\(slang\) "Cooker \(slang\)")" in Australia.[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-162) The term "cooker" is also loosely associated with the [far right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Australia "Far-right politics in Australia").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-163)[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-164)
## Politics
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/911worldopinionpoll_Sep2008.png/500px-911worldopinionpoll_Sep2008.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:911worldopinionpoll_Sep2008.png)A [2008 poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polls_about_9/11_conspiracy_theories "Opinion polls about 9/11 conspiracy theories") found that majorities in only 9 of 17 countries believed that [al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda "Al-Qaeda") carried out the [9/11 attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_attacks "9/11 attacks").[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-165)
The philosopher [Karl Popper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Popper "Karl Popper") described the central problem of conspiracy theories as a form of [fundamental attribution error](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundamental_attribution_error "Fundamental attribution error"), where every event is generally perceived as being intentional and planned, greatly underestimating the effects of randomness and unintended consequences.[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAm2013-101) In his book _[The Open Society and Its Enemies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Open_Society_and_Its_Enemies "The Open Society and Its Enemies")_ , he used the term "the conspiracy theory of society" to denote the idea that social phenomena such as "war, unemployment, poverty, shortages ... [are] the result of direct design by some powerful individuals and groups".[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-166) Popper argued that [totalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism "Totalitarianism") was founded on conspiracy theories which drew on imaginary plots which were driven by paranoid scenarios predicated on [tribalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribalism "Tribalism"), [chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chauvinism "Chauvinism"), or [racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism "Racism"). He also noted that conspirators very rarely achieved their goal.[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-dohloo-167)
Historically, real conspiracies have usually had little effect on history and have had [unforeseen consequences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unforeseen_consequence "Unforeseen consequence") for the conspirators, in contrast to conspiracy theories, which often posit grand, sinister organizations or world-changing events, the evidence for which has been erased or obscured.[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Cumings1999-168)[[166]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-SciAmShermer-169) As described by [Bruce Cumings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Cumings "Bruce Cumings"), history is instead "moved by the broad forces and large structures of human collectivities".[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Cumings1999-168)
### Arab world
Main article: [Conspiracy theories in the Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories_in_the_Arab_world "Conspiracy theories in the Arab world")
Conspiracy theories are a prevalent feature of [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab "Arab") culture and politics.[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Gray-170) Variants include conspiracies involving colonialism, [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism "Zionism"), superpowers, oil, and the [war on terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_terrorism "War on terrorism"), which is often referred to in Arab media as a "[war against Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_against_Islam "War against Islam")".[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Gray-170) For example, _[The Protocols of the Elders of Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protocols_of_the_Elders_of_Zion "The Protocols of the Elders of Zion")_ , an infamous [hoax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoax "Hoax") document purporting to be a Jewish plan for world domination, is commonly read and promoted in the Muslim world.[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-171)[[169]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-172)[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-173) [Roger Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Cohen "Roger Cohen") has suggested that the popularity of conspiracy theories in the Arab world is "the ultimate refuge of the powerless".[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Cohen-137) Al-Mumin Said has noted the danger of such theories, for they "keep us not only from the truth but also from confronting our faults and problems".[[171]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-174) [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden "Osama bin Laden") and [Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_al-Zawahiri "Ayman al-Zawahiri") used conspiracy theories about the United States to gain support for [al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda "Al-Qaeda") in the Arab world, and as rhetoric to distinguish themselves from similar groups, although they may not have believed the conspiratorial claims themselves.[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Gray158-159-175)
### Turkey
Main article: [Conspiracy theories in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories_in_Turkey "Conspiracy theories in Turkey")
Conspiracy theories are a prevalent feature of culture and politics in [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey "Turkey"). Conspiracism is an important phenomenon in understanding Turkish politics.[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-tinfoil-176) This is explained by a desire to "make up for our lost [Ottoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire "Ottoman Empire") grandeur",[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-tinfoil-176) the humiliation of perceiving Turkey as part of "the malfunctioning half" of the world,[[174]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-177) and a "low level of media literacy among the Turkish population."[[175]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-178)
There are a wide variety of conspiracy theories including the [Judeo-Masonic conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Masonic_conspiracy_theory "Judeo-Masonic conspiracy theory"),[[176]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-179)[[177]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-180) the [international Jewish conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Jewish_conspiracy#Turkey "International Jewish conspiracy"), and the [war against Islam conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_against_Islam_conspiracy_theory "War against Islam conspiracy theory"). For example, [Islamists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamists "Islamists"), dissatisfied with the [modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernity "Modernity") and [secularist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularist "Secularist") reforms that took place throughout the history of the Ottoman Empire and the Turkish Republic, have put forward many conspiracy theories to defame the [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne "Treaty of Lausanne"), an important peace treaty for the country, and the republic's founder [Kemal Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk "Kemal Atatürk").[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-181)[[179]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-182) Another example is the [Sèvres syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A8vres_syndrome "Sèvres syndrome"), a reference to the [Treaty of Sèvres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_S%C3%A8vres "Treaty of Sèvres") of 1920, a popular belief in Turkey that dangerous internal and external enemies, especially [the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world "Western world"), are "conspiring to weaken and carve up the Turkish Republic".[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-183)
### United States
Main article: [Conspiracy theories in United States politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories_in_United_States_politics "Conspiracy theories in United States politics")
The historian [Richard Hofstadter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Hofstadter "Richard Hofstadter") addressed the role of [paranoia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paranoia "Paranoia") and conspiracism throughout [U.S. history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._history "U.S. history") in his 1964 essay "[The Paranoid Style in American Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Paranoid_Style_in_American_Politics "The Paranoid Style in American Politics")". [Bernard Bailyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Bailyn "Bernard Bailyn")'s classic _[The Ideological Origins of the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ideological_Origins_of_the_American_Revolution "The Ideological Origins of the American Revolution")_ (1967) notes that a similar phenomenon could be found in North America during the time preceding the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution "American Revolution"). Conspiracism labels people's attitudes and the type of conspiracy theories that are more global and historical in proportion.[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-184)
Harry G. West and others have noted that while conspiracy theorists may often be dismissed as a fringe minority, certain evidence suggests that a wide range of the U.S. believes in conspiracy theories. West also compares those theories to [hypernationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypernationalism "Hypernationalism") and [religious fundamentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_fundamentalism "Religious fundamentalism").[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-185)[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-186) Theologian Robert Jewett and philosopher [John Shelton Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Shelton_Lawrence "John Shelton Lawrence") attribute the enduring popularity of conspiracy theories in the U.S. to the [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War"), [McCarthyism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McCarthyism "McCarthyism"), and [counterculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterculture_of_the_1960s "Counterculture of the 1960s") rejection of authority. They state that among both the left-wing and right-wing, there remains a willingness to use real events, such as Soviet plots, inconsistencies in the [Warren Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Report "Warren Report"), and the [9/11 attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_attacks "9/11 attacks"), to support the existence of unverified and ongoing large-scale conspiracies.[[184]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-187)
In his studies of "American political demonology," historian [Michael Paul Rogin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Paul_Rogin "Michael Paul Rogin") too analyzed this paranoid style of politics that has occurred throughout American history. Conspiracy theories frequently identify an imaginary subversive group that is supposedly attacking the nation and requires the government and allied forces to engage in harsh extra-legal repression of those threatening subversives. Rogin cites examples from the Red Scares of 1919 to McCarthy's anti-communist campaign in the 1950s and, more recently, fears of immigrant hordes invading the U.S. Unlike Hofstadter, Rogin saw these "countersubversive" fears as frequently coming from those in power and dominant groups instead of from the dispossessed. Unlike Robert Jewett, Rogin blamed not the counterculture but America's dominant culture of liberal individualism and the fears it stimulated to explain the periodic eruption of irrational conspiracy theories.[[185]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-188) The [Watergate scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_scandal "Watergate scandal") has also been used to bestow legitimacy to other conspiracy theories, with [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon "Richard Nixon") himself commenting that it served as a "[Rorschach ink blot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rorschach_ink_blot "Rorschach ink blot")" which invited others to fill in the underlying pattern.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-knight-2003-61)
Historian Kathryn S. Olmsted cites three reasons why Americans are prone to believing in government conspiracy theories: 
  1. Genuine government overreach and secrecy during the Cold War, such as [Watergate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate "Watergate"), the [Tuskegee syphilis experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_syphilis_experiment "Tuskegee syphilis experiment"), [Project MKUltra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_MKUltra "Project MKUltra"), and the CIA's [assassination attempts on Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_attempts_on_Fidel_Castro "Assassination attempts on Fidel Castro") in collaboration with mobsters.
  2. Precedent set by official government-sanctioned conspiracy theories for propaganda, such as claims of German infiltration of the U.S. during World War II or the debunked claim that [Saddam Hussein played a role in the 9/11 attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein_and_al-Qaeda_link_allegations "Saddam Hussein and al-Qaeda link allegations").
  3. Distrust fostered by the government's spying on and harassment of dissenters, such as the [Sedition Act of 1918](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedition_Act_of_1918 "Sedition Act of 1918"), [COINTELPRO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COINTELPRO "COINTELPRO"), and as part of various [Red Scares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Scare "Red Scare").[[186]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-189)


[Alex Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Jones "Alex Jones") referenced numerous conspiracy theories for convincing his supporters to endorse [Ron Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Paul "Ron Paul") over [Mitt Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney "Mitt Romney") in the [2012 Republican Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries "2012 Republican Party presidential primaries") and [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") over [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton "Hillary Clinton") in the [2016 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election "2016 United States presidential election").[[187]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-190)[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-191) Into the 2020s, the [QAnon conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QAnon_conspiracy_theory "QAnon conspiracy theory") alleges that Trump is fighting against a [deep-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_state_in_the_United_States "Deep state in the United States") [cabal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabal "Cabal") of child sex-abusing and Satan-worshipping [Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(United_States\) "Democratic Party \(United States\)").[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Nature_2021-37)[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-Crossley_2021-38)[[189]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-192)[[190]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-193)[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-194)[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory#cite_note-195)
## See also
  * [Apophenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apophenia "Apophenia") – Tendency to perceive connections between unrelated things
  * [Big lie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_lie "Big lie") – Propaganda technique
  * [Conspiracy fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_fiction "Conspiracy fiction") – Subgenre of thriller fiction
  * [Disinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinformation "Disinformation") – Deliberately misleading information
  * [Fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news "Fake news") – False or misleading information presented as real
  * [Fringe theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fringe_theory "Fringe theory") – Idea which departs from accepted scholarship in the field
  * [Furtive fallacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Furtive_fallacy "Furtive fallacy") – Informal fallacy of emphasis
  * [List of conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conspiracy_theories "List of conspiracy theories")
  * [Philosophy of conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_conspiracy_theories "Philosophy of conspiracy theories") – Branch of academic study
  * [Propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda "Propaganda") – Communication used to influence opinion
  * [Pseudohistory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudohistory "Pseudohistory") – Pseudoscholarship that attempts to distort historical record

Retrieved 24 April 2024. "It also seems to be the case that the way we normally use the term conspiracy theory excludes instances where the theory has been generally accepted as true...Just as the Watergate scandal is now part of the official account of the Nixon administration, the NSA monitoring practices are arguably also part of our present understanding of the way that US intelligence works and neither thus qualify as 'conspiracy theories' anymore. The point here is that when we employ the term 'conspiracy theory' in actual language use, we are implicitly assuming and implying that the claims advanced by the theory are not true."
